=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})\}
in (
3
) and (
4
).

The successive pairs of compatible (resp. closed-compatible) multi-designs stay compatible (resp. closed-compatible) after one step of the definition, thus this is well defined. Moreover, if
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
are closed-compatible then, according to the base case,
{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}
will be a closed design.
In particular, if
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
{\mathfrak{n}}
are atomic designs then
{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}|{\mathfrak{n}}}={\mathfrak{p}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x_{0}]
.

In order to prove an
associativity
theorem for multi-designs, recall first the original theorem on designs:

Theorem 50

(Associativity)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a design and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{n}}_{k}
be negative designs.

(\![{\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}/y_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{n}}_{k}/y_{k}]]\!)=(\![(\![{\mathfrak{d}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{n}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]]\!).

This result was first established by Girard
[
11
]
. The theorem, in the form given above, was proved by Basaldella and Terui
[
4
]
. Associativity naturally extends to multi-designs as follows:

Theorem 51

(Multi-associativity)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
be a multi-design and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{n}}_{k}
be negative designs.

(\![{\mathfrak{D}}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}/y_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{n}}_{k}/y_{k}]]\!)=(\![(\![{\mathfrak{D}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{n}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]]\!).

Proof.

Immediate from the definition of the normal form of a multi-design (Definition
46
) and simple associativity (Theorem
50
).
∎

Corollary 52
.

(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}]\!)=(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}]\!)}]\!)

Proof.

By induction on
{\mathfrak{E}}
:

•

If
{\mathfrak{E}}=\emptyset
then
(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|\emptyset}]\!)=(\![{\mathfrak{D}}]\!)=(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}]\!)|\emptyset}]\!)=(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}]\!)|(\![\emptyset]\!)}]\!)
.

•

If
{\mathfrak{E}}={\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}\}
, let
S=\{{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}/y_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}/y_{k}\}=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{p}})\}
.
By definitions of the normal form of multi-designs (Definition
46
) and of
\mathfrak{Cut}_{.|.}
(Definition
49
), and using associativity (Theorem
51
), we have:

\displaystyle(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}]\!)

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}[S]\}|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}]\!)

by Def.
49

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{(\![({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}[S]\}]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S]\!)\cup\{(\![(\![{\mathfrak{p}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]]\!)\}|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by Def.
46
and Thm.
51

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{(\![(\![{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S]\!)\cup\{(\![{\mathfrak{p}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]\}]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by Def.
46

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S]\!)\cup\{(\![{\mathfrak{p}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]\}|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}]\!)}]\!)

by Def.
46
and
49

•

If
{\mathfrak{E}}={\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\}
with
x\notin\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})
, similar as above with
S=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})\}
.

•

If
{\mathfrak{E}}={\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\}
with
x\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})
, let
S=\{{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}/y_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}/y_{k}\}=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})\}
.
We have:

\displaystyle(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}]\!)

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)[{\mathfrak{n}}[S]/x]|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}]\!)

by Def.
49

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{(\![({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)[{\mathfrak{n}}[S]/x]]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{(\![(\![{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S]\!)[(\![(\![{\mathfrak{n}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]]\!)/x]]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by using Thm.
51
twice

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{(\![(\![{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{n}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]/x]]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by Thm.
51

\displaystyle=(\![{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{\{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{n}}]\!)[(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}]\!)/y_{1},\dots,(\![{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}]\!)/y_{k}]/x]|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}]\!)}]\!)

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{(\![{\mathfrak{D}}]\!)|(\![{\mathfrak{E}}]\!)}]\!)

by Def.
46
and
49

∎

Lemma 53
.

{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}={\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the number
n
of variables in
(\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})\cap\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{E}}))\cup(\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{E}})\cap\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{D}}))
.

•

If
n=0
then
{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}={\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}={\mathfrak{D}}\cup{\mathfrak{E}}
.

•

Let
n>0
and suppose the property is satisfied for all
k<n
. Without loss of generality suppose there exists
x\in(\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})\cap\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{E}}))
. Thus
{\mathfrak{E}}
is of the form
{\mathfrak{E}}={\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\}
. Let
S=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})\}
.

–

If
S=\emptyset
, let
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}
be the design such that
x\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{d}})
, and let us write
{\mathfrak{D}}={\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{d}}\}
. If
{\mathfrak{d}}
is positive then:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x]\}|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}

by one step
4
of Def.
49

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x]\}}

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\setminus T^{\prime})\cup\{{\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x,T^{\prime}]\}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}

by one step
2
of Def.
49

where
T^{\prime}=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x])\}
. Let
T=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{E}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{d}})\}
, we have
T=T^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\}
, indeed:
\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x])=(\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{d}})\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})
, where
\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})\cap\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{E}})=\emptyset
by definition of a multi-design, thus also
\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})\cap\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Therefore:

\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\setminus T^{\prime})\cup\{{\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x,T^{\prime}]\}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus T)\cup\{{\mathfrak{d}}[T]\}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}

by one step
2
of Def.
49
backwards, hence the result. The reasoning is similar if
{\mathfrak{d}}
is negative and
{\mathfrak{D}}={\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{d}}/y\}
, we just have to distinguish between the cases
y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime})
and
y\notin\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime})
.

–

Otherwise, let
S^{\prime}=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{E}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}(S)\}
and
S^{\prime\prime}=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}(S^{\prime})\}
; note that
S^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}\subseteq({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)
. We have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime})\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}[S[S^{\prime}]]\}|{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S}

by several steps
4
of Def.
49

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S|({\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime})\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}[S[S^{\prime}]]\}}

\displaystyle\mbox{ by induction hypothesis, since }S\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus(S\cup S^{\prime\prime}))[{\mathfrak{n}}[S[S^{\prime}[S^{\prime\prime}]]]/x]|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime}}

by one step
4
of Def.
49

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}

by steps
4
of Def.
49
backwards

The last equality is obtained by moving successively, from left to right, all the designs from
S^{\prime}
, and finally the design
{\mathfrak{n}}
.

∎

Lemma 54
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
be multi-designs such that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}
is a multi-design, and
{\mathfrak{E}}
is compatible with
{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}
. We have:

\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}|{\mathfrak{E}}}=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}|\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}}}

Proof.

By induction on
{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}
:

•

If
{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}=\emptyset
then
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}}={\mathfrak{E}}
hence the result.

•

If
{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}={\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}\}
then
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}}=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus S)\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}[S]\}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}}
where
S=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{E}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{p}})\}
. Thus by induction hypothesis:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}|\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}|({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus S)\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}[S]\}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{(({\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime})\setminus S^{\prime})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}[S[S^{\prime}]]\}|{\mathfrak{E}}\setminus S}

by one step
2
of Def.
49

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{(({\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2})\setminus S^{\prime})[S[S^{\prime}]]|{\mathfrak{E}}\setminus S}

where
S^{\prime}=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in({\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2})\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}(S)\}
.
Finally, by several steps
4
backwards of Definition
49
, this is equal to
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}|{\mathfrak{E}}}
.

•

If
{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}={\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\}
and
x\notin\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{E}})
, then similar to previous case.

•

If
{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}={\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\}
and
x\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{E}})
, then
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}}=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus S)[{\mathfrak{n}}[S]/x]|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}}
where
S=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{E}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}})\}
. Thus by induction hypothesis:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}|\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}|({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus S)[{\mathfrak{n}}[S]/x]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus S)[{\mathfrak{n}}[S]/x]|{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{\prime}}

by Lemma
53

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}}

by one step
4
backwards of Def.
49

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}|{\mathfrak{E}}}

by Lemma
53

∎

We now extend the notion of orthogonality to multi-designs.

Definition 55
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
be closed-compatible multi-designs.
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
are
orthogonal
, written
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
, if
{\mathfrak{Cut}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}\Downarrow\maltese
.

A.2
Paths and Multi-Designs

Recall that we write
\epsilon
for the empty sequence.

Definition 56
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
be a multi-design.

•

A
view of

{\mathfrak{D}}
is a view of a design in
{\mathfrak{D}}
.

•

A
path of

{\mathfrak{D}}
is a path
\mathpzc{s}
of same polarity as
{\mathfrak{D}}
such that for all prefix
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
of
\mathpzc{s}
,
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}
.

We are now interested in a particular form of closed interactions, where we can identify two sides of the multi-design: designs are separated into two groups such that there are no cuts between designs of the same group. This corresponds exactly to the interaction between two closed-compatible multi-designs.

Definition 57
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
be closed-compatible multi-designs such that
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
. The
interaction path
of
{\mathfrak{D}}
with
{\mathfrak{E}}
is the unique path
\mathpzc{s}
of
{\mathfrak{D}}
such that
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{E}}
.

But nothing ensures the existence and uniqueness of such a path: this will be proved in the rest of this subsection. We will moreover show that, if
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
, this path corresponds to the
interaction sequence
defined below. For the purpose of giving an inductive definition of the interaction sequence, we define it not only for a pair of closed-compatible multi-designs but for a larger class of pairs of multi-designs.

Definition 58
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
be multi-designs of opposite polarities, compatible, and satisfying
\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{E}})
and
\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{E}})\subseteq\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{D}})
. The
interaction sequence
of
{\mathfrak{D}}
with
{\mathfrak{E}}
, written
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
, is the sequence of actions followed by interaction on the side of
{\mathfrak{D}}
. More precisely, if we write
{\mathfrak{p}}
for the only positive design of
{\mathfrak{D}}\cup{\mathfrak{E}}
, the interaction sequence is defined recursively by:

•

If
{\mathfrak{p}}=\maltese
then:

–

\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\maltese
if
\maltese\in{\mathfrak{D}}

–

\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\epsilon
if
\maltese\in{\mathfrak{E}}
.

•

If
{\mathfrak{p}}=\Omega
then
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\epsilon
.

•

If
{\mathfrak{p}}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}}\rangle
then there exists
{\mathfrak{n}}
such that
({\mathfrak{n}}/x)\in{\mathfrak{E}}
if
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}
,
({\mathfrak{n}}/x)\in{\mathfrak{D}}
otherwise. Let us write
{\mathfrak{n}}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{S}}b(\overrightarrow{y^{b}}).{\mathfrak{p}}_{b}
. We have
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\kappa\langle{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\rangle
where:

–

if
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}
, then
\kappa=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{y^{a}}\rangle
,
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}/y^{a}}\}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}={({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}}
.

–

otherwise
\kappa=a_{x}(\overrightarrow{y^{a}})
,
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}/y^{a}}\}
.

Note that this applies in particular to two closed-compatible multi-designs. Remark also that this definition follows exactly the interaction between
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
: indeed, in the inductive case of the definition, the multi-designs
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}
are obtained from
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
similarly to the following lemma. In particular the interaction sequence is finite whenever the interaction between
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
is finite.

Lemma 59
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
be closed-compatible multi-designs of opposite polarities. Suppose the only positive design
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}
is of the form
{\mathfrak{p}}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{n}}}\rangle
, and suppose moreover there exists
{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}
such that
({\mathfrak{n}}_{0}/x)\in{\mathfrak{E}}
, say
{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{S}}b(\overrightarrow{x^{b}}).{\mathfrak{p}}_{b}
. Then:

\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}\leadsto\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}\setminus\{({\mathfrak{m}}/x_{i}^{a})\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ x_{i}^{a}\notin\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{p}}_{a})\}

where
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{n}}/x^{a}}\}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
.

Proof.

First notice that as
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
are closed-compatible,
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}
is a design, and since this design has cuts we can indeed apply one step of reduction to it. Let
S^{\prime}=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/x_{i}^{a})\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ x_{i}^{a}\notin\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{p}}_{a})\})
. We have to prove
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}\leadsto\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}\setminus S^{\prime}
. The proof is done by induction on the number of designs in
{\mathfrak{E}}
.

•

If
{\mathfrak{E}}=\{{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}/x\}
, then
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}=\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
.
In this case let
S=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}}_{0})\}
, and remark that, as
{\mathfrak{E}}
and
{\mathfrak{D}}
are closed-compatible,
S={\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\}
.
Thus:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)[{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}[S]/x]|\emptyset}

by one step
4
of Def.
49

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{p}}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}[S]/x]

\displaystyle\leadsto{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}[S][\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{n}}/x^{a}}]

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{p}}_{a}[{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}[{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime}_{0}]\}\cup S^{\prime}_{0}\setminus S^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mbox{where }S^{\prime}_{0}=\mathinner{\mathchoice{S^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}{S^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}{S^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}{S^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{S^{\prime}_{0}\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}[{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime}_{0}]\}|\emptyset}\setminus S^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}}\setminus S^{\prime}

by one step
2
backwards of Def.
49

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}\setminus S^{\prime}

•

Otherwise there exists
({\mathfrak{n}}_{1}/z)\in{\mathfrak{E}}
such that
x\neq z
. Suppose
z\notin\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})
(resp.
z\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})
).
Define:

–

S=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}}_{1})\}
, and remark that
S=\{({\mathfrak{m}}/y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{n}}_{1})\}
.

–

{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\setminus S)\cup\{({\mathfrak{n}}_{1}[S]/z)\}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\setminus S)[{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}[S]/z]
)

–

{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime\prime}={\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\setminus\{({\mathfrak{n}}_{1}/z)\}
.

We have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)\cup\{({\mathfrak{n}}_{1}[S]/z)\}|{\mathfrak{E}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}/z\}}

by one step
3
of Def.
49

(   resp.

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus S)[({\mathfrak{n}}_{1}[S]/z)]|{\mathfrak{E}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}/z\}}

by one step
4
of Def.
49
)

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime\prime}}\setminus S^{\prime}

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}\setminus S^{\prime}

by step
3
(resp.
4
) of Def.
49
backwards

∎

Lemma 60
.

If
\maltese\in(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}]\!)
(in particular if
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
) then
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\makebox[32.22229pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.5pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}$}}
. Otherwise
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\overline{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}
.

Proof.

It is clear from the definition of the interaction sequence that the proper actions in
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
are the opposite of those in
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle
. Concerning the daimon: since the interaction sequence follows the interaction between
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
,
\maltese
appears at the end of one of the sequences
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
or
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle
if and only if
\maltese\in(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{D}}|{\mathfrak{E}}}]\!)
, and in this case
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\makebox[32.22229pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.5pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle}}$}}
.
∎

Proposition 61
.

Every positive-ended prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
is a path of
{\mathfrak{D}}
. In particular, if
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
is finite and positive-ended then it is a path of
{\mathfrak{D}}
.

Proof.

First remark that every (finite) prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
is an aj-sequence. Indeed, since
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
are well shaped multi-designs the definition of interaction sequence ensures that an action cannot appear before its justification, and all the conditions of the definition of an aj-sequence are satisfied:
Alternation
and
Daimon
are immediate from the definition of interaction sequence, while
Linearity
is indeed satisfied as variables are disjoint in
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
(by Barendregt’s convention).

By definition, for every prefix
\mathpzc{s}
of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
,
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view. We show that it is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}
by induction on the length of
\mathpzc{s}
:

•

If
\mathpzc{s}=\epsilon
then
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\epsilon}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}=\epsilon
is indeed a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}
.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}=\maltese
then
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\maltese
. From the definition of interaction sequence, we know that in this case
\maltese\in{\mathfrak{D}}
, hence
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\maltese}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\urcorner$}=\maltese
is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}
.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}=\kappa\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
where
\kappa
is proper, then
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle=\kappa\langle{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}
are as in Definition
58
, and
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is a prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\rangle
. By induction hypothesis,
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
. Two possibilities:

–

Either
\kappa=\kappa^{+}
is positive.
From the definition of the interaction sequence, this means
{\mathfrak{p}}:=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}}\rangle\in{\mathfrak{D}}
,
\kappa^{+}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{y^{a}}\rangle
and
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}/y^{a}}\}
. We have
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}=\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\kappa^{+}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\urcorner$}
and either
\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\kappa^{+}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\urcorner$}=\kappa^{+}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
if the first negative action of
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is justified by
\kappa^{+}
(i.e.,
\exists i
such that
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}/y^{a}_{i}
), or
\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\kappa^{+}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\urcorner$}=\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
otherwise (i.e.,
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\}
). In the second case, there is nothing more to show; in the first one, by definition of the views of a design,
\kappa^{+}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{p}}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}}\rangle
.

–

Or
\kappa=\kappa^{-}
is negative. Hence there exists a design
{\mathfrak{n}}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{S}}b(\overrightarrow{y^{b}}).{\mathfrak{p}}_{b}
such that
({\mathfrak{n}}/x)\in{\mathfrak{D}}
,
\kappa^{-}=a_{x}(\overrightarrow{y^{a}})
, and
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
. We have
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}=\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\kappa^{-}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
and either
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\kappa^{-}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}=\kappa^{-}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
if the first action of
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is positive (i.e.,
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}
), or
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\kappa^{-}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}=\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
otherwise (i.e.,
\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}\subseteq{\mathfrak{D}}
). In the second case, there is nothing to do; in the first one, note that
\kappa^{-}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\raisebox{2.59543pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
({\mathfrak{n}}/x)
, hence the result.

We have proved that
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}
. This implies in particular that
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
satisfies P-visibility, indeed: given a prefix
\mathpzc{s}\kappa^{+}
of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
, the action
\kappa^{+}
is either initial or it is justified in
\mathpzc{s}
by the same action that justifies it in
{\mathfrak{D}}
; since
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{D}}
, the justification of
\kappa^{+}
is in it, thus P-visibility is satisfied. Similarly, we can prove that
\raisebox{1.2284pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{t}}\raisebox{1.2284pt}{$\urcorner$}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{E}}
whenever
\mathpzc{t}
is a prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle
, therefore
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle
also satisfies P-visibility; by Lemma
60
either
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle=\makebox[32.22229pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.5pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.5pt}{$\resizebox{2111720}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle}}$}}
or
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle=\overline{\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle}
, thus this implies that
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
satisfies O-visibility. Hence every positive-ended prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
is a path, and since the views of its prefixes are views of
{\mathfrak{D}}
, it is a path of
{\mathfrak{D}}
.
∎

Remark
.

If
\mathpzc{s}\kappa_{1}^{+}
and
\mathpzc{s}\kappa_{2}^{+}
are views (resp. paths) of a multi-design
{\mathfrak{D}}
then
\kappa_{1}^{+}=\kappa_{2}^{+}
. Indeed, if
\mathpzc{s}\kappa_{1}^{+}
and
\mathpzc{s}\kappa_{2}^{+}
are views of
{\mathfrak{D}}
, the result is immediate by definition of the views of a design; if they are paths of
{\mathfrak{D}}
, just remark that
\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}\kappa_{1}^{+}}\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\urcorner$}=\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}\kappa_{1}^{+}
and
\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}\kappa_{2}^{+}}\raisebox{3.03987pt}{$\urcorner$}=\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\urcorner$}\kappa_{2}^{+}
are views of
{\mathfrak{D}}
, hence the conclusion.

Proposition 62
.

Suppose
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
,
\mathpzc{s}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
\overline{\mathpzc{s}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{E}}
. The path
\mathpzc{s}
is a prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathpzc{s}
is not a prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
. Let
\mathpzc{t}
be the longest common prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
and
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
(possibly
\epsilon
). Without loss of generality, we can assume there exist actions of same polarity
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
such that
\kappa_{1}\neq\kappa_{2}
,
\mathpzc{t}\kappa_{1}
is a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
and
\mathpzc{t}\kappa_{2}
is a prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
: indeed, if there are no such actions, it is because
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
is a strict prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
; in this case, it suffices to consider
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle
and
\overline{\mathpzc{s}}
instead.

•

If
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are positive, then
\mathpzc{t}\kappa_{1}
and
\mathpzc{t}\kappa_{2}
are paths of
{\mathfrak{D}}
, and by previous remark
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
: contradiction.

•

If
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are negative, a contradiction arises similarly from the fact that
\overline{\mathpzc{t}\kappa_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathpzc{t}\kappa_{2}}
are paths of
{\mathfrak{E}}
where
\overline{\kappa_{1}}
and
\overline{\kappa_{2}}
are positive.

Hence the result.
∎

The following result ensures that the interaction path is well defined.

Proposition 63
.

If
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
, there exists a unique path
\mathpzc{s}
of
{\mathfrak{D}}
such that
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{E}}
.

Proof.

Lemma
60
and Proposition
61
show that such a path exists, namely
\langle{\mathfrak{D}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle
. Unicity follows from Proposition
62
.
∎

Conversely, we prove that the existence of such a path implies the orthogonality of multi-designs (Proposition
65
).

Proposition 64
.

Let
{\mathfrak{P}}
and
{\mathfrak{N}}
be closed-compatible multi-designs such that
\Omega\notin{\mathfrak{P}}
and such that their interaction is finite.
Suppose that for every path
\mathpzc{s}\kappa^{+}
of
{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\kappa^{+}
is proper and
\overline{\mathpzc{s}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{N}}
,
\overline{\mathpzc{s}\kappa^{+}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{N}}
, and suppose also that the same condition is satisfied when reversing
{\mathfrak{P}}
and
{\mathfrak{N}}
. Then
{\mathfrak{P}}\perp{\mathfrak{N}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the number
n
of steps of the interaction before divergence/convergence:

•

If
n=0
, then we must have
{\mathfrak{P}}=\maltese
, since
\Omega\notin{\mathfrak{P}}
. Hence the result.

•

If
n>0
then
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
is of the form
{\mathfrak{p}}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{n}}}\rangle
and there exists
{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{S}}b(\overrightarrow{x^{b}}).{\mathfrak{p}}_{b}
such that
({\mathfrak{n}}_{0}/x)\in{\mathfrak{N}}
. Let
\kappa^{+}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{x^{a}}\rangle
and remark that
\kappa^{+}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{p}}
. By hypothesis,
\overline{\kappa^{+}}=a_{x}(\overrightarrow{x^{a}})
is a path of
{\mathfrak{N}}
, thus a path of
{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}
, and this implies
{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\neq\Omega
. By Lemma
59
, we have
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{P}}|{\mathfrak{N}}}\leadsto\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{P}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime}}\setminus\{({\mathfrak{m}}/x^{a}_{i})\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ x_{i}\notin\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{p}}_{a})\}
where
{\mathfrak{P}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{P}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{n}}/x^{a}}\}
and
{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{N}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
. This corresponds to a cut-net between two closed-compatible multi-designs
{\mathfrak{P}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{P}}^{\prime}
(negative) and
{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime}
(positive), where:

–

\Omega\notin{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime\prime}
because
{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\neq\Omega
;

–

their interaction is finite and takes
n-1
steps;

–

the condition on paths stated in the proposition is satisfied for
{\mathfrak{P}}^{\prime\prime}
and
{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime\prime}
, because it is for
{\mathfrak{P}}
and
{\mathfrak{N}}
: indeed, the paths of
{\mathfrak{P}}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime\prime}
) are the paths
\mathpzc{t}
such that
\kappa^{+}\mathpzc{t}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{P}}
(resp.
\overline{\kappa^{+}}\mathpzc{t}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{N}}
), unless such a path
\mathpzc{t}
contains a negative initial action whose address is not the address of a positive action on the other side, but this restriction is harmless with respect to our condition.

We apply the induction hypothesis to get
{\mathfrak{P}}^{\prime\prime}\perp{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime\prime}
. Finally
{\mathfrak{P}}\perp{\mathfrak{N}}
.

∎

Proposition 65
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}
be closed-compatible multi-designs.
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
if and only if there exists a path
\mathpzc{s}
of
{\mathfrak{D}}
such that
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{E}}
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
If
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp{\mathfrak{E}}
then result follows from Proposition
63
.

(\Leftarrow)
We will prove that the hypothesis of Proposition
64
is satisfied. Let us show that every path of
{\mathfrak{D}}
(resp. of
{\mathfrak{E}}
) of the form
\mathpzc{t}\kappa^{+}
where
\kappa^{+}
is proper and
\overline{\mathpzc{t}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{E}}
(resp. of
{\mathfrak{D}}
) is a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
(resp. of
\overline{\mathpzc{s}}
). By induction on the length of
\mathpzc{t}
, knowing that it is either empty or negative-ended:

•

If
\mathpzc{t}
is empty,
\kappa^{+}
is necessarily the first action of the positive design in
{\mathfrak{D}}
(resp. in
{\mathfrak{E}}
), hence the first action of
\mathpzc{s}
(resp. of
\overline{\mathpzc{s}}
).

•

If
\mathpzc{t}=\mathpzc{t}_{0}\kappa^{-}
, then
\overline{\mathpzc{t}_{0}\kappa^{-}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{E}}
(resp. of
{\mathfrak{D}}
) and
\mathpzc{t}_{0}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{D}}
(resp. of
{\mathfrak{E}}
). By induction hypothesis,
\overline{\mathpzc{t}}=\overline{\mathpzc{t}_{0}\kappa^{-}}
is a prefix of
\overline{\mathpzc{s}}
(resp. of
\mathpzc{s}
), thus
\mathpzc{t}
is a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
(resp. of
\overline{\mathpzc{s}}
). The path
\mathpzc{s}
is of the form
\mathpzc{s}=\mathpzc{t}\kappa^{\prime+}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
. But since
\mathpzc{s}
and
\mathpzc{t}\kappa^{+}
are both paths of
{\mathfrak{D}}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{E}}
), they cannot differ on a positive action, hence
\kappa^{+}=\kappa^{\prime+}
. Thus
\mathpzc{t}\kappa^{+}
is a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
.

∎

A.3
Associativity for Interaction Paths

If
\mathpzc{s}
is a path of a multi-design
{\mathfrak{D}}
, and
{\mathfrak{E}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{D}}
, then we write
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}
for the longest subsequence of
\mathpzc{s}
that is a path of
{\mathfrak{E}}
. Note that this is well defined.

Proposition 66

(Associativity for paths)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{D}}
,
{\mathfrak{E}}
and
{\mathfrak{F}}
be cut-free multi-designs such that
{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}
is a multi-design, and suppose
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp({\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}})
. We have:

\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}

Proof.

We will prove the result for a larger class of multi-designs. Instead of the assumption
{\mathfrak{D}}\perp({\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}})
, suppose that
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}
are:

•

of opposite polarities

•

compatible

•

satisfying
\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}})
and
\mathrm{fv}({\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}})\subseteq\mathrm{np}({\mathfrak{D}})

•

and such that
\maltese\in(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)
(in particular their interaction is finite).

First remark that
{\mathfrak{F}}
and
{\mathfrak{D}}
are compatible, hence it is possible to define
\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}
. Then since
\maltese\in(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)
, we have
\maltese\in(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)}]\!)
, indeed:

\displaystyle(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)

\displaystyle=(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}}]\!)

by Lemmas
54
and
53

\displaystyle=(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{E}}|(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)}]\!)

by Corollary
52

This also shows that
{\mathfrak{E}}
and
(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)
are compatible. As they are of opposite polarities and they satisfy the condition on variables,
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle
is defined.

Let
\mathpzc{s}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}\rangle
, and let us show the result (i.e.,
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle
) by induction on the length of
\mathpzc{s}
, which is finite because the interaction between
{\mathfrak{D}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}
is finite.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}=\epsilon
then necessarily
\maltese\in{\mathfrak{D}}
thus also
\maltese\in(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)
. Hence
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\epsilon=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle
.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}=\maltese
then
\maltese\in{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}
. If
\maltese\in{\mathfrak{E}}
then
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle=\maltese=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}
. Otherwise
\maltese\in{\mathfrak{F}}
, thus
\maltese\in(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)
and
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle=\epsilon=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}
.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}=\kappa^{+}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
where
\kappa^{+}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{x^{a}}\rangle
is a proper positive action, then
{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}
is a positive multi-design such that its only positive design is of the form
{\mathfrak{p}}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}}\rangle
. Thus
{\mathfrak{D}}
is negative, and there exists
{\mathfrak{n}}
such that
({\mathfrak{n}}/x)\in{\mathfrak{D}}
of the form
{\mathfrak{n}}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{S}}b(\overrightarrow{x^{b}}).{\mathfrak{p}}_{b}
, where
{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\neq\Omega
because the interaction converges. Let
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
.

–

Either
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
[
reduction step
].

In this case, we have
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}
, so let us show that
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle
. By definition of the interaction sequence, we have
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{({\mathfrak{m}}/x^{a})}\}
. Thus by induction hypothesis
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\rangle
. But by Lemma
59
,
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\rangle=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle
because the negatives among
\overrightarrow{({\mathfrak{m}}/x^{a})}
in
(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)
will not interfere in the interaction with
{\mathfrak{E}}
, since the variables
\overrightarrow{x^{a}}
do not appear in
{\mathfrak{E}}
. Hence the result.

–

Or
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}
[
commutation step
].

In this case, we have
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\kappa^{+}(\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}})
, and by definition of the interaction sequence
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{({\mathfrak{m}}/x^{a})}\}
. Thus by induction hypothesis
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\rangle
.
But we have

\displaystyle\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\cup\{({\mathfrak{n}}/x)\}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}}]\!)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\cup\{({\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}/x)\}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{a}\}\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa^{+}\langle{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\rangle

where
{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}
is the only negative design of
(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)
on variable
x
, and
{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}^{\prime}
the only positive design of
(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)
.
Hence
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle=\kappa^{+}(\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}})=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}
.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}=\kappa^{-}\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
where
\kappa^{-}=a_{x}(\overrightarrow{x^{a}})
, then
{\mathfrak{D}}
is positive with only positive design of the form
{\mathfrak{p}}=x|\overline{a}\langle\overrightarrow{{\mathfrak{m}}}\rangle
, and there exists a negative design
{\mathfrak{n}}
such that
({\mathfrak{n}}/x)\in{\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}}
, with
{\mathfrak{n}}
of the form
{\mathfrak{n}}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{S}}b(\overrightarrow{x^{b}}).{\mathfrak{p}}_{b}
where
{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\neq\Omega
. By definition of the interaction sequence, we have
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}=\langle(({\mathfrak{E}}\cup{\mathfrak{F}})\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{D}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}\})\cup\{\overrightarrow{({\mathfrak{m}}/x^{a})}\}
.

–

Either
{\mathfrak{n}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
[
reduction step
].

In this case, we have
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}
, so let us show that
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle
. By induction hypothesis
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
, and by Lemma
59
we deduce
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle
, hence the result.

–

Or
{\mathfrak{n}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}
[
commutation step
].

In this case, we have
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\kappa^{-}(\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}})
. By induction hypothesis
\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}}}=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{E}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{n}}/x\})\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{a}\}
.
But we have

\displaystyle\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}\}\setminus\{\overrightarrow{({\mathfrak{m}}/x^{a})}\}}]\!)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\cup\{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\}\setminus\{\overrightarrow{({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}/x^{a})}\}\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-}\langle{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)\rangle

where
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
is the only positive design of
(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)
, and for each
i\leq ar(a)
,
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}_{i}
is the only negative design of
(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}}]\!)
on variable
x^{a}_{i}
.
Therefore
\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\leftarrow(\![\mathfrak{Cut}_{{\mathfrak{F}}|{\mathfrak{D}}}]\!)\rangle=\kappa^{-}(\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}})=\mathinner{\mathchoice{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\mkern 1.0mu\upharpoonright\mkern 1.0mu{\mathfrak{E}}}{\mathpzc{s}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!{\mathfrak{E}}}}
, which concludes the proof.

∎

Appendix B
Proofs of Subsection
3.2

We now come back to (non “multi-”) designs, and we prove:

•

the form of visitable paths for each connective (§
B.2
), which is needed for next point;

•

that (some) connectives preserve regularity (Propositions
84
,
87
,
88
, corresponding to Proposition
18
), purity (Proposition
19
) and quasi-purity (Proposition
21
).

B.1
Preliminaries

B.1.1
Observational Ordering and Monotonicity

We consider the
observational ordering

\preceq
over designs:

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\preceq{\mathfrak{d}}
if
{\mathfrak{d}}
can be obtained from
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}
by substituting:

•

positive subdesigns for some occurrences of
\Omega
.

•

\maltese
for some positive subdesigns.

Remark in particular that for all positive designs
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
, we have
\Omega\preceq{\mathfrak{p}}\preceq\maltese
, and if
{\mathfrak{p}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
then
{\mathfrak{p}}\preceq{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. We can now state the
monotonicity
theorem, an important result of ludics. A proof of the theorem formulated in this form is found in
[
17
]
.

Theorem 67

(Monotonicity)
.

•

If
{\mathfrak{d}}\preceq{\mathfrak{e}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\preceq{\mathfrak{n}}
, then
{\mathfrak{d}}[{\mathfrak{m}}/x]\preceq{\mathfrak{e}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x]

•

If
{\mathfrak{d}}\preceq{\mathfrak{e}}
then
(\![{\mathfrak{d}}]\!)\preceq(\![{\mathfrak{e}}]\!)

This means that the relation
\preceq
compares the likelihood of convergence: if
{\mathfrak{d}}\perp{\mathfrak{e}}
and
{\mathfrak{d}}\preceq{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}
then
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\perp{\mathfrak{e}}
. In particular, if
{\mathbf{B}}
is a behaviour, if
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathbf{B}}
and
{\mathfrak{d}}\preceq{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}
then
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
.

Remark the following important fact: given a path
\mathpzc{s}
of some design
{\mathfrak{d}}
, there is a unique design maximal for
\preceq
such that
\mathpzc{s}
is a path of it. Indeed, this design
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner\mkern-6.0mu\ulcorner\mkern-2.0mu$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\mkern-2.0mu\urcorner\mkern-6.0mu\urcorner$}^{c}
is obtained from
{\mathfrak{d}}
by replacing all positive subdesigns (possibly
\Omega
) whose first positive action is not in
\mathpzc{s}
by
\maltese
. Note that, actually, the design
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner\mkern-6.0mu\ulcorner\mkern-2.0mu$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\mkern-2.0mu\urcorner\mkern-6.0mu\urcorner$}^{c}
does not depend on
{\mathfrak{d}}
but only on the path
\mathpzc{s}
.

Example 68
.

Consider design
{\mathfrak{d}}
and the path
\mathpzc{s}
below:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{d}}\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle=\hskip 14.22636ptx|\overline{a}\langle b(y).(y|\overline{e}\langle\rangle),c().\maltese+d(z).(z|\overline{e}\langle\rangle)\rangle

\displaystyle\mathpzc{s}\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle=\hskip 14.22636ptx|\overline{a}\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle\hskip 28.45274ptb_{x_{1}}(y)\hskip 28.45274pty|\overline{e}\langle\rangle\hskip 28.45274ptc_{x_{2}}()\hskip 28.45274pt\maltese

x_{0}|\overline{a}\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle
d_{x_{2}}(y)
z|\overline{e}\langle\rangle
c_{x_{2}}()
\maltese
b_{x_{1}}(y)
y|\overline{e}\langle\rangle
\mathpzc{s}

We have
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner\mkern-6.0mu\ulcorner\mkern-2.0mu$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\mkern-2.0mu\urcorner\mkern-6.0mu\urcorner$}^{c}=x|\overline{a}\langle b(y).(y|\overline{e}\langle\rangle)+\sum_{f\neq b}f(\overrightarrow{x^{f}}).\maltese,\sum_{f\in\mathcal{S}}f(\overrightarrow{x^{f}}).\maltese\rangle
x_{0}|\overline{a}\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle
\dots
f_{x_{2}}(\overrightarrow{x^{f}})
\maltese
\dots
d_{x_{2}}(y)
\maltese
c_{x_{2}}()
\maltese
b_{x_{1}}(y)
y|\overline{e}\langle\rangle
\dots
f_{x_{1}}(\overrightarrow{x^{f}})
\maltese
\dots
\mathpzc{s}

Proposition 69
.

If
\mathpzc{s}\in V_{{\mathbf{{\mathbf{B}}}}}
then
\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner\mkern-6.0mu\ulcorner\mkern-2.0mu$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\mkern-2.0mu\urcorner\mkern-6.0mu\urcorner$}^{c}\in{\mathbf{B}}
.

Proof.

There exists
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathbf{B}}
such that
\mathpzc{s}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
, thus
{\mathfrak{d}}\preceq\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\ulcorner\mkern-6.0mu\ulcorner\mkern-2.0mu$}{\mathpzc{s}}\raisebox{-0.61685pt}{$\mkern-2.0mu\urcorner\mkern-6.0mu\urcorner$}^{c}
. The result then comes from monotonicity (Theorem
67
).
∎

B.1.2
More on Paths

Let
{\mathbf{B}}
be a behaviour.

Lemma 70
.

If
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathbf{B}}
and
\mathpzc{s}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
, then
\mathpzc{s}
is a path of
|{\mathfrak{d}}|
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}
such that
\mathpzc{s}=\langle{\mathfrak{d}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}\rangle
, and let
\mathpzc{t}=\langle|{\mathfrak{d}}|\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}\rangle
.

•

Since
|{\mathfrak{d}}|\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{d}}
, the path
\mathpzc{s}
cannot be a strict prefix of
\mathpzc{t}
, and
\mathpzc{s}
and
\mathpzc{t}
cannot differ on a positive action.

•

If
\mathpzc{t}
is a strict prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
then it is positive-ended. So
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{6.15079pt}{$\resizebox{254863}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{t}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{6.15079pt}{$\resizebox{254863}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{t}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{6.15079pt}{$\resizebox{254863}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{t}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{6.15079pt}{$\resizebox{254863}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{t}}}
are paths of
{\mathfrak{e}}
differing on a positive action, which is impossible.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}
and
\mathpzc{t}
differ on a negative action, say
\mathpzc{u}\kappa^{-}_{1}
and
\mathpzc{u}\kappa^{-}_{2}
are respective prefixes of
\mathpzc{s}
and
\mathpzc{t}
with
\kappa^{-}_{1}\neq\kappa^{-}_{2}
, then
\overline{\mathpzc{u}\kappa^{-}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathpzc{u}\kappa^{-}_{2}}
are paths of
{\mathfrak{e}}
differing on a positive action, which is impossible.

Thus we must have
\mathpzc{s}=\mathpzc{t}
, hence the result.
∎

Lemma 71
.

Let
\mathpzc{s}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
. For every positive-ended (resp. negative-ended) prefix
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
of
\mathpzc{s}
, we have
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
(resp.
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\maltese\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
).

Proof.

Let
\mathpzc{s}=\langle{\mathfrak{d}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{d}}\in B
and
{\mathfrak{e}}\in B^{\perp}
, and let
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
be a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is negative-ended, let
\kappa^{+}
be such that
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa^{+}
is a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
. The action
\kappa^{+}
comes from
{\mathfrak{d}}
. Consider design
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}
obtained from
{\mathfrak{d}}
by replacing the positive subdesign of
{\mathfrak{d}}
starting on action
\kappa^{+}
with
\maltese
. Since
{\mathfrak{d}}\preceq{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}
, by monotonicity
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
. Moreover
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\maltese=\langle{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}\rangle
, hence the result.

•

If
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is positive-ended then either
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}=\mathpzc{s}
and there is nothing to prove or
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is a strict prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
, so assume we are in the second case.
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is
\maltese
-free, hence
\overline{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}
is a negative-ended prefix of
\makebox[3.94444pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{4.30554pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{4.30554pt}{$\resizebox{258503}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}}}$}}\in V_{{\mathbf{B^{\perp}}}}
. Using the argument above, it comes
\makebox[6.75pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.51782pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}$}}=\overline{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\maltese\in V_{{\mathbf{B^{\perp}}}}
, thus
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
.

∎

Lemma 72
.

Let
\mathpzc{s}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
. For every prefix
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa^{-}
of
\mathpzc{s}
and every
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathbf{B}}
such that
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
,
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa^{-}
is a prefix of a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
.

Proof.

There exist
{\mathfrak{d}}_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}
such that
\mathpzc{s}=\langle{\mathfrak{d}}_{0}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\rangle
. Let
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa^{-}
be a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
, and let
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathbf{B}}
such that
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
. Since
\overline{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}
is a prefix of a path of
{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}
,
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}
is a prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{d}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\rangle
. We cannot have
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}=\langle{\mathfrak{d}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\rangle
, otherwise
\makebox[6.75pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.51782pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.51782pt}{$\resizebox{442368}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}}$}}=\overline{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}}\maltese
and
\overline{\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa^{-}}
would be paths of
{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}
differing on a positive action, which is impossible.
Thus there exists
\kappa^{\prime-}
such that
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime-}
is a prefix of
\langle{\mathfrak{d}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\rangle
, which is a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
, and necessarily
\kappa^{-}=\kappa^{\prime-}
. Finally
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa^{-}
is a prefix of a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
.
∎

B.1.3
An Alternative Definition of Regularity

Define the
anti-shuffle
(

\shuffle

) as the dual operation of shuffle, that is:

if
S
and
T
are sets of paths of same polarity.

Definition 73
.

•

A
trivial view
is an aj-sequence such that each proper action except the first one is justified by the immediate previous action. In other words, it is a view such that its dual is a view as well.

•

The
trivial view of
an aj-sequence is defined inductively by:

\displaystyle\langle\epsilon\rangle

\displaystyle=\epsilon

empty sequence

\displaystyle\langle\mathpzc{s}\maltese\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathpzc{s}\rangle\maltese

\displaystyle\langle\mathpzc{s}\kappa\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }\kappa\neq\maltese\mbox{ initial}

\displaystyle\langle\mathpzc{s}\kappa\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathpzc{s}_{0}\rangle\kappa

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }\kappa\neq\maltese\mbox{ justified, where }\mathpzc{s}_{0}\mbox{ prefix of }\mathpzc{s}\mbox{ ending on }\mathrm{just}(\kappa)

We also write
\langle\kappa\rangle_{\mathpzc{s}}
(or even
\langle\kappa\rangle
) instead of
\langle\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa\rangle
when
\mathpzc{s}^{\prime}\kappa
is a prefix of
\mathpzc{s}
.

•

Trivial views of a design

{\mathfrak{d}}
are the trivial views of its paths (or of its views). In particular,
\epsilon
is a trivial view of negative designs only.

•

Trivial views of designs in
|{\mathbf{B}}|
are called
trivial views of

{\mathbf{B}}
.

Lemma 74
.

1.

Every view is in the anti-shuffle of trivial views.

2.

Every path is in the shuffle of views.

Proof.

1.

Let
{\mathbb{v}}
be a view, the result is shown by induction on
{\mathbb{v}}
:

•

If
{\mathbb{v}}=\epsilon
or
{\mathbb{v}}=\kappa
, it is itself a trivial view, hence the result.

•

Suppose
{\mathbb{v}}={\mathbb{v}}^{\prime}\kappa
with
{\mathbb{v}}^{\prime}\neq\epsilon
and
{\mathbb{v}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{t}}_{1}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}\dots\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}{\mathbb{t}}_{n}
where the
{\mathbb{t}}_{i}
are trivial views.

–

If
\kappa
is negative,
as
{\mathbb{v}}
is a view, the action
\kappa
is justified by the last action of
{\mathbb{v}}^{\prime}
, say
\kappa^{+}
. Hence
\kappa^{+}
is the last action of some trivial view
{\mathbb{t}}_{i_{0}}
.
Hence
{\mathbb{v}}\in{\mathbb{t}}_{1}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}\dots\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}{\mathbb{t}}_{i_{0}-1}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}({\mathbb{t}}_{i_{0}}\kappa)\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}{\mathbb{t}}_{i_{0}+1}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}\dots\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}{\mathbb{t}}_{n}
.

–

If
\kappa
is positive, either it is initial and
{\mathbb{v}}\in{\mathbb{t}}_{1}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}\dots\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}{\mathbb{t}}_{n}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}\kappa
with
\kappa
a trivial view, or it is justified by
\kappa^{-}
in
{\mathbb{v}}^{\prime}
. In the last case, there exists a unique
i_{0}
such that
\kappa^{-}
appears in
{\mathbb{t}}_{i_{0}}
, so let
{\mathbb{t}}\kappa^{-}
be the prefix of
{\mathbb{t}}_{i_{0}}
ending with
\kappa^{-}
. We have that
{\mathbb{v}}\in{\mathbb{t}}_{1}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}\dots\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}{\mathbb{t}}_{n}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}({\mathbb{t}}\kappa^{-}\kappa)
where
{\mathbb{t}}\kappa^{-}\kappa
is a trivial view.

2.

Similar reasoning as above, but replacing

\shuffle

by
\shuffle
, “trivial view” by “view”, “view” by “path”, and exchanging the role of the polarities of actions.

∎

Remark
.

Following previous result, note that every view (resp. path) of a design
{\mathfrak{d}}
is in the anti-shuffle of trivial views (resp. in the shuffle of views) of
{\mathfrak{d}}
.

Proposition 75
.

{\mathbf{B}}
is regular if and only if the following conditions hold:

•

the positive-ended trivial views of
{\mathbf{B}}
are visitable in
{\mathbf{B}}
,

•

V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
and
V_{{\mathbf{B^{\perp}}}}
are stable under
\shuffle
(i.e.,
V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
is stable under
\shuffle
and

\shuffle

).

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{B}}
be a behaviour.

(
\Rightarrow
) Suppose
{\mathbf{B}}
is regular, and let
{\mathbb{t}}
be a positive-ended trivial view of
{\mathbf{B}}
. There exists a view
{\mathbb{v}}
of a design
{\mathfrak{d}}\in|{\mathbf{B}}|
such that
{\mathbb{t}}
is a subsequence of
{\mathbb{v}}
, and such that
{\mathbb{v}}
ends with the same action as
{\mathbb{t}}
. Since
{\mathbb{v}}
is a view of
{\mathfrak{d}}
,
{\mathbb{v}}
is in particular a path of
{\mathfrak{d}}
, hence by regularity
{\mathbb{v}}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
. There exists
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}
such that
{\mathbb{v}}=\langle{\mathfrak{d}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{e}}\rangle
, and by Lemma
70
we can even take
{\mathfrak{e}}\in|{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}|
. Since
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}
is a path of
{\mathfrak{e}}
,
\ulcorner
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}
\urcorner
is a view of
{\mathfrak{e}}
. But notice that
\raisebox{2.0776pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\makebox[7.5pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.0pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{v}}}}$}}}\raisebox{2.0776pt}{$\urcorner$}=\raisebox{2.0776pt}{$\ulcorner$}{\makebox[7.5pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.0pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}$}}}\raisebox{2.0776pt}{$\urcorner$}=\makebox[7.5pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.0pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}$}}
by definition of a view and of a trivial view. We deduce that
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}
is a view (and in particular a path) of
{\mathfrak{e}}
, hence
\makebox[7.5pt][c]{\mbox{\rule{0.0pt}{7.0pt}$\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{7.0pt}{$\resizebox{491520}{0}{$\sim$}$}\hss}{{\mathbb{t}}}}$}}\in V_{{\mathbf{B^{\perp}}}}
by regularity. Finally,
{\mathbb{t}}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Assume the two conditions of the statement.
Let
\mathpzc{s}
be a path of some design of
|{\mathbf{B}}|
. By Lemma
74
, we know that there exist views
{\mathbb{v}}_{1}
, …,
{\mathbb{v}}_{n}
such that
\mathpzc{s}\in{\mathbb{v}}_{1}\shuffle\dots\shuffle{\mathbb{v}}_{n}
, and for each
{\mathbb{v}}_{i}
there exist trivial views
{\mathbb{t}}_{i,1}
, …,
{\mathbb{t}}_{i,m_{i}}
such that
{\mathbb{v}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{t}}_{i,1}\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}\dots\text{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\shuffle$}}{\mathbb{t}}_{i,m_{i}}
.
By hypothesis each
{\mathbb{t}}_{i,j}
is visitable in
{\mathbf{B}}
, hence as
V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
is stable by anti-shuffle,
{\mathbb{v}}_{i}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
. Thus as
V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
is stable by shuffle,
\mathpzc{s}\in V_{{\mathbf{B}}}
.
Similarly the paths of designs of
|{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}|
are visitable in
{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}
. Hence the result.
∎

B.2
Form of the Visitable Paths

From internal completeness, we can make explicit the form of the visitable paths for behaviours constructed by logical connectives; such results are necessary for proving the stability of regularity and purity (§
B.3
and
B.4
respectively).

We will use the notations given at the beginning of Subsection
2.2
, and also the following. Given an action
\kappa
and a set of sequences
V
, we write
\kappa V
for
\{\kappa\mathpzc{s}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{|}\penalty\ \mathpzc{s}\in V\}
. Let us note
\kappa_{\blacktriangledown}=x_{0}|\blacktriangledown\langle x\rangle
,
\kappa_{\blacktriangle}=\blacktriangle_{x_{0}}(x)
,
\kappa_{\bullet}=x_{0}|\bullet\langle x,y\rangle
and
\kappa_{\iota_{i}}=x_{0}|\iota_{i}\langle x_{i}\rangle
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

In this section are proved the following results:

•

V_{{\mathbf{\shpos N}}}=\kappa_{\blacktriangledown}V_{{\mathbf{N}}}^{x}\cup\{\maltese\}
and
V_{{\mathbf{\shneg P}}}=\kappa_{\blacktriangle}V_{{\mathbf{P}}}^{x}\cup\{\epsilon\}
(Proposition
78
),

•

V_{{\mathbf{M\oplus N}}}=\kappa_{\iota_{1}}V_{{\mathbf{M}}}^{x_{1}}\cup\kappa_{\iota_{2}}V_{{\mathbf{N}}}^{x_{2}}\cup\{\maltese\}
(Proposition
79
),

•

V_{{\mathbf{M\otimes N}}}=\kappa_{\bullet}(V_{{\mathbf{M}}}^{x}\shuffle V_{{\mathbf{N}}}^{y})\cup\{\maltese\}
if
{\mathbf{M}}
and
{\mathbf{N}}
are regular (Proposition
82
),

•

the general form of the visitable paths of
{\mathbf{M}}\otimes{\mathbf{N}}
, not as simple (Proposition
80
),

•

finally, the case of
\multimap
easily deduced from
\otimes
(Corollaries
81
and
83
).

B.2.1
Shifts

Lemma 76
.

1.

(\blacktriangle(x).({\mathbf{N}}^{\perp})^{x})^{\perp}\subseteq\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathbf{N}}\rangle\cup\{\maltese\}
.

2.

\blacktriangle(x).({\mathbf{N}}^{\perp})^{x}\subseteq\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathbf{N}}\rangle^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Let
E=\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathbf{N}}\rangle
, and let
F=\blacktriangle(x).({\mathbf{N}}^{\perp})^{x}
. To show the lemma, we must show
F^{\perp}\subseteq E\cup\{\maltese\}
and
F\subseteq E^{\perp}
.

1.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}\in F^{\perp}
. If
{\mathfrak{q}}\neq\maltese
,
{\mathfrak{q}}
is necessarily of the form
\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle
where
{\mathfrak{n}}
is a negative atomic design. For every design
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbf{N}}^{\perp}
, we have
\blacktriangle(x).{\mathfrak{p}}^{x}\in F
and
{\mathfrak{q}}[\blacktriangle(x).{\mathfrak{p}}^{x}/x_{0}]\leadsto{\mathfrak{p}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x_{0}]
, thus
(\![{\mathfrak{q}}[\blacktriangle(x).{\mathfrak{p}}^{x}/x_{0}]]\!)=(\![{\mathfrak{p}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x_{0}]]\!)=\maltese
since
{\mathfrak{q}}\perp\blacktriangle(x).{\mathfrak{p}}^{x}
. We deduce
{\mathfrak{n}}\in{\mathbf{N}}
, hence
{\mathfrak{q}}\in E
.

2.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}=\blacktriangle(x).{\mathfrak{p}}^{x}\in F
. For every design
{\mathfrak{n}}\in{\mathbf{N}}
, we have
\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle[{\mathfrak{m}}/x_{0}]\leadsto{\mathfrak{p}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x_{0}]
, thus
(\![\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle[{\mathfrak{m}}/x_{0}]]\!)=(\![{\mathfrak{p}}[{\mathfrak{n}}/x_{0}]]\!)=\maltese
since
{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbf{N}}^{\perp}
and
{\mathfrak{n}}\in{\mathbf{N}}
. Hence
{\mathfrak{m}}\in E^{\perp}
.

∎

Lemma 77
.

\shneg{\mathbf{P}}=(\shpos{\mathbf{P}}^{\perp})^{\perp}
.

Proof.

If we take
{\mathbf{N}}={\mathbf{P}}^{\perp}
, Lemma
76
gives us:

1.

(\blacktriangle(x).{\mathbf{P}}^{x})^{\perp}\subseteq\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathbf{P}}^{\perp}\rangle\cup\{\maltese\}
and

2.

\blacktriangle(x).{\mathbf{P}}^{x}\subseteq\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathbf{P}}^{\perp}\rangle^{\perp}
.

Let
E=\blacktriangledown\langle{\mathbf{P}}^{\perp}\rangle
, and let
F=\blacktriangle(x).{\mathbf{P}}^{x}
. By definition
